Even on a quiet day when I'm supposed to be relaxing, I’m bouncing from the garden club list to my knitting bag, and then over to my travel papers before I’ve really finished any one thing. \n\nActually, the garden club is planning a big hydrangea sale this spring. Hydrangeas are just so lovely, but they need the exact right soil acidity, you know? My neighbor Susan tried planting them last year and they turned completely pink when she wanted blue. I told her she needed to save her morning coffee grounds for the soil, but she never listens. Oh, and the travel papers—my sister wants to go to Branson, Missouri. Have you ever been to Branson? I hear the shows are wonderful, but I just don't know if I can sit through a long bus ride to get down there...\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this energetic and restless throughout your life, or is this something that has only started happening more recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's definitely not new. Goodness, no. Even way back in grade school, my teachers used to tell my mother that I was bright, but just so scattered. I was always losing my school papers, talking out of turn, and wiggling around in my seat before I ever finished my work. \n\nI remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable... oh, she had these thick, horn-rimmed glasses and she kept a big glass jar of peppermints right on her desk. Not the soft peppermint puffs, but the hard little discs wrapped in that crinkly clear plastic. I just loved those. I think she gave them out if you got a perfect score on your spelling test, but I never got one because I'd always rush through and misspell something silly like 'receive.' You know the rule, 'i before e except after c,' but honestly, who can remember all those rules when you're just looking out the window waiting for recess? We had this giant metal slide on the playground that would get absolutely scorching hot in the afternoon sun, you had to be so careful or it would burn the back of your legs...\n\nDoctor: Have there been periods in your life lasting several days or longer where you felt unusually 'high,' irritable, or significantly more energetic than your usual baseline self?\n\nPatient: Unusually high or... no, I wouldn't say that. I don't think I've ever had days where I felt different or more energetic than my regular self, because I've just always been this busy, you know? I'm just my normal self all the time. \n\nThough speaking of energy, I did try to channel some of mine into organizing the attic last Tuesday. My daughter thinks I keep too many things, but you just never know when something will come in handy! Like, I found my whole old collection of glass Mason jars tucked away in a box up there. I used to do a lot of canning when the children were little. Peaches, mostly. We had a wonderful little peach tree at our old house on Elm Street. My husband used to make the most incredible peach cobbler with them... he'd put just a tiny pinch of nutmeg right into the crust. It sounds odd, but oh, it was absolutely delicious. You have to be so careful with nutmeg, though. Just a little too much and it completely overpowers the whole dish. I was looking at the spices at the grocery store just the other day, and goodness, the prices they want for a tiny little bottle of nutmeg now! It’s highway robbery, really. I remember when a dollar could get you a whole bag of groceries, and now you can barely buy a candy bar...\n\nDoctor: Does this tendency to be scattered and restless cause you significant trouble or frustration in your day-to-day life, such as in your relationships or your ability to manage your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it does cause some trouble. It's actually been causing some real strain at home lately, and down at the community center, too. My husband gets so frustrated with me, and the other volunteers... well, they feel like they just can't count on me to keep our plans straight unless someone is constantly checking up behind me to make sure I actually did what I said I would. \n\nLike last week, I was supposed to bring the paper plates for the community center potluck. We were having a big spaghetti dinner to raise money for the new playground equipment. I remember writing \"plates\" on a little yellow sticky note, but then the phone rang and it was my neighbor Susan again. She was going on and on about how the mail carrier keeps walking across her lawn instead of using the sidewalk. She's very particular about her grass, Susan is. She uses this special fertilizer that smells just awful for three days after she puts it down. I always have to keep my windows shut when she does it. Anyway, I completely forgot the sticky note, and when I showed up to the dinner with a lovely green bean casserole but no plates... oh, it was a mess. People were trying to eat spaghetti out of little paper cups meant for the punch! Have you ever tried to twirl spaghetti in a punch cup? It just doesn't work at all...\n\nDoctor: Beyond being restless and always on the move, do you often find that you interrupt others while they are talking or have difficulty waiting for your turn in conversations or group activities?\n\nPatient: Oh, interrupting... well, yes, I suppose I do. My husband is always telling me I cut him off right in the middle of a sentence. And I do the same thing down at the community center when we're trying to plan things. I just... if I don't say the thought the exact second it pops into my head, it just vanishes completely! It's like trying to hold onto water with your bare hands. Speaking of water, our kitchen faucet has been dripping for three days now. Drip, drip, drip. It's driving me absolutely batty. My husband says he's going to fix it, but you know how that goes. He needs to go to the hardware store for a little rubber washer, but the hardware store on Main Street closed down last month. Such a shame. Mr. Henderson owned that place for forty years. He always wore those neat little bow ties, every single day. I don't know where men even buy bow ties anymore. You certainly don't see them at the big department stores at the mall...\n\nDoctor: When you look back at your childhood and your daily life now, would you say that your difficulty with staying focused on tasks and forgetting things is more of a problem for you than the constant physical restlessness and tendency to interrupt others?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure which one is worse, to be honest. I mean, they both happen, but picking one... I don't know. The forgetting things is definitely frustrating, like I was saying about the plates. But then the restlessness... oh, I just can't sit still. Like when I try to watch television in the evenings. We were watching that nice program about the British baking competition last night, and I just had to get up and start wiping down the baseboards in the living room. You wouldn't believe how much dust collects on those baseboards! It's because of our golden retriever, Buster. He sheds like you wouldn't believe. I brush him every single morning out on the back patio, but it just doesn't seem to make a dent. The hair just floats around like dandelion seeds. Oh, I used to love blowing dandelion seeds when I was a little girl. We had a whole field of them behind our house in Ohio. My sister Margaret and I would make little flower crowns out of them before they went to seed. Margaret lives in Florida now, near Sarasota. She says the humidity is terrible for her arthritis, but she loves being near the ocean...\n\nDoctor: If you had to choose just one, which do you feel causes more significant problems in your daily life: the struggle to focus and keep track of your responsibilities, or the constant need to be moving and the tendency to interrupt others?\n\nPatient: Oh, I really couldn't say. Choosing just one... I don't think I can. They both just cause their own sorts of messes, so I really don't know which one is worse. It's a bit like comparing apples and oranges, isn't it? \n\nOh, speaking of oranges, I bought a bag of those lovely little clementines at the market on Tuesday. You know, the ones that come in those little wooden crates? I always save those crates because they're just so perfect for organizing things. I tried using one to sort my sewing thread last week. I have so many spools of thread, mostly leftover from when I used to make my daughter's Halloween costumes. She was a ladybug one year, with these big red wings that I sewed black felt spots onto. Felt is just so expensive nowadays, though. I went to the craft store last month and nearly had a heart attack at the prices. And the line at the checkout took forever because the cashier's machine ran out of receipt paper...\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these difficulties happen at home and at the community center; do you find that you have these same struggles with focus and restlessness when you are in other situations as well, such as when you are out running errands, visiting friends, or attending appointments?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... yes, it happens everywhere, really. When I'm out running errands or... well, just last week I was at the dentist's office for my regular cleaning. Dr. Miller is such a nice man, but his waiting room chairs are terribly uncomfortable. They're that stiff vinyl material that sticks to the back of your legs if you're wearing a skirt. I was trying to read a magazine while I waited—one of those home decorating ones—but I just couldn't keep my mind on the article about window treatments. I kept getting up to look at the fish tank they have in the corner. They have this one little yellow fish that just darts around endlessly. It reminded me of a canary my grandmother used to keep in her kitchen. She named him Peep, which I always thought was a bit unoriginal for a bird, don't you think? She kept him in this beautiful ornate brass cage right next to the window where she grew African violets. I've never had any luck with African violets myself. I always seem to overwater them, and then the leaves get all mushy and rot. It's so disappointing when you try so hard to take care of a plant and it just dies anyway...\n\nDoctor: If you look back at the past six months, would you say that you have experienced more days where you were primarily struggling to pay attention and stay on task, or more days where you were primarily struggling with being physically restless and unable to control your impulses?\n\nPatient: Well, looking back over the last six months... I really don't think I could say if one happens more often than the other. I'm just not sure. It feels like every day is a mix of both, really. Some days I can't focus, some days I can't sit still, and most days it's a bit of everything. \n\nLike yesterday, for instance. I was trying to sit down and finally write out my Christmas cards. I know it's a bit early, but if I don't start now, they'll never get sent out in time. I bought these beautiful cards from a little boutique downtown—they have a lovely snowy village scene on the front with little specks of glitter that catch the light. But the glitter gets absolutely everywhere! I found a piece on Buster's nose this morning. He looked so funny, just sniffing around with a sparkly nose. He loves the snow, you know. Whenever it snows, he just buries his whole face in it and runs around the yard like a puppy, even though he's eight years old now. Dog years are so strange to think about. I read somewhere that the whole \"seven years for every human year\" thing isn't actually accurate, and it depends on the breed and size of the dog. Buster is a big boy, about seventy-five pounds, so I suppose he's getting up there in age, just like me. My knees certainly feel like they're in dog years sometimes... especially when it rains. The weatherman said it's supposed to rain all weekend, which is a shame because the neighbor's boy was going to come over and help me rake the leaves...\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, before you turned twelve, did you have trouble staying seated or finishing your schoolwork because you were constantly moving or distracted?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. Even way back in grade school, my teachers always told my mother that I was bright but just so scattered. I was always losing my papers, talking out of turn, and just wiggling around in my seat before I ever finished my work. My third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she had this massive beehive hairdo that must have taken a whole can of hairspray to keep up every morning—she was always sending little notes home to my mother about my fidgeting. \n\nMy mother actually kept all those notes. She had them folded up in this old floral tin box that originally held Scottish shortbread cookies. Oh, I absolutely love shortbread cookies, don't you? They are just wonderful with a good cup of tea in the afternoon. I usually have Earl Grey, but my neighbor Brenda insists that peppermint tea is the only thing you should drink after lunch for your digestion. Brenda has terrible digestion, poor thing. She can't even eat a hint of onion without getting awful heartburn. We went to that new Italian place on Main Street for lunch last week, and she made the waiter go back to the kitchen three times to make absolutely sure there wasn't any garlic or onion in her marinara sauce. I felt so awful for the young man, he looked like he was only about sixteen and just terrified of her...\n\nDoctor: When you think about your daily life now, would you say that you struggle with both being easily distracted and being physically restless, or is one of those two problems significantly more frequent than the other?\n\nPatient: Well, I think... didn't we just... well, it really is both, just like I was saying before. I couldn't possibly pick one over the other. It’s just a jumble of both every single day. \n\nLike this morning, I finally sat down in my armchair to watch the morning news, but I just couldn't stay put. I kept getting up to fiddle with the thermostat. My son installed one of those new digital ones for me—he means well, bless his heart, but the numbers are so tiny! I have to hunt down my reading glasses just to turn the heat up two degrees. Anyway, I couldn't sit still through the local broadcast, and then I completely missed the weather report because I got distracted by a smudge on the television screen. I tried to wipe it off with a tissue, but you know those tissues with the aloe lotion in them? They leave the most terrible streaks on glass. I only buy them because my nose gets so raw from my allergies. The goldenrod is just awful right now. Brenda, my neighbor, she actually put artificial flowers out on her front porch just to avoid the pollen. But honestly, I think fake flowers just look a bit tacky, and they collect so much dust. Have you ever tried to dust a fake plastic fern? It’s practically impossible. You have to take the whole thing into the shower and rinse it off, and then the water gets all over the bathroom floor...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

You have to take the whole thing into the shower and rinse it off, and then the water gets all over the bathroom floor...\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there other specific settings, such as at a doctor's office, a friend's house, or a social gathering, where these struggles with focus and restlessness cause you the same kind of trouble you experience at home?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"It has started causing real strain at home and at the community center, because my husband and other volunteers can’t count on me to keep plans straight without someone checking behind me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, definitely, it’s not just sitting in my own living room. It has actually started causing some real strain at home and over at the community center, too. It’s gotten to the point where my husband and the other volunteers just can’t count on me to keep our plans straight without someone checking behind me. 

Like just last Tuesday, I was supposed to organize the refreshment table for the morning knitting circle. I used to knit all the time, you know. I made the most beautiful baby blankets whenever someone in the neighborhood was expecting. I always used that soft pastel yellow yarn because you never know if they’re having a boy or a girl, and I think yellow is just such a cheerful color. It reminds me of daffodils. We had a whole row of daffodils along the front walkway at our old house, but the squirrels kept digging up the bulbs! They are such menaces, those squirrels. I tried sprinkling cayenne pepper on the dirt because I read in a magazine that it keeps them away, but the wind just blew it right back into my eyes. Oh, it stung terribly! I had to flush my eyes out with cold water for twenty minutes. 

Anyway, my husband gets so frustrated with me now because he feels like he has to supervise me, and the ladies at the center had to scramble to get the coffee made themselves because I was supposed to set it up, but I just kept wandering around the hall looking at the bulletin boards and completely lost track of what I was doing...

Goodness, no. Even way back in grade school, my teachers used to tell my mother that I was bright, but just so scattered. My husband is always telling me I cut him off right in the middle of a sentence. And I do the same thing down at the community center when we're trying to plan things. I just... if I don't say the thought the exact second it pops into my head, it just vanishes completely! It's like trying to hold onto water with your bare hands. Speaking of water, our kitchen faucet has been dripping for three days now. Drip, drip, drip. It's driving me absolutely batty. My husband says he's going to fix it, but you know how that goes. He needs to go to the hardware store for a little rubber washer, but the hardware store on Main Street closed down last month. Such a shame. Mr. Henderson owned that place for forty years. He always wore those neat little bow ties, every single day. I don't know where men even buy bow ties anymore. You certainly don't see them at the big department stores at the mall...\n\nDoctor: When you look back at your childhood and your daily life now, would you say that your difficulty with staying focused on tasks and forgetting things is more of a problem for you than the constant physical restlessness and tendency to interrupt others?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure which one is worse, to be honest. I mean, they both happen, but picking one... I don't know. The forgetting things is definitely frustrating, like I was saying about the plates. But then the restlessness... oh, I just can't sit still. Like when I try to watch television in the evenings. We were watching that nice program about the British baking competition last night, and I just had to get up and start wiping down the baseboards in the living room. I brush him every single morning out on the back patio, but it just doesn't seem to make a dent. The hair just floats around like dandelion seeds. Oh, I used to love blowing dandelion seeds when I was a little girl. We had a whole field of them behind our house in Ohio. My sister Margaret and I would make little flower crowns out of them before they went to seed. Margaret lives in Florida now, near Sarasota. She says the humidity is terrible for her arthritis, but she loves being near the ocean...\n\nDoctor: If you had to choose just one, which do you feel causes more significant problems in your daily life: the struggle to focus and keep track of your responsibilities, or the constant need to be moving and the tendency to interrupt others?\n\nPatient: Oh, I really couldn't say. I have so many spools of thread, mostly leftover from when I used to make my daughter's Halloween costumes. She was a ladybug one year, with these big red wings that I sewed black felt spots onto. And the line at the checkout took forever because the cashier's machine ran out of receipt paper...\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these difficulties happen at home and at the community center; do you find that you have these same struggles with focus and restlessness when you are in other situations as well, such as when you are out running errands, visiting friends, or attending appointments?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... yes, it happens everywhere, really. I was trying to read a magazine while I waited—one of those home decorating ones—but I just couldn't keep my mind on the article about window treatments. I kept getting up to look at the fish tank they have in the corner. They have this one little yellow fish that just darts around endlessly. It reminded me of a canary my grandmother used to keep in her kitchen. She named him Peep, which I always thought was a bit unoriginal for a bird, don't you think? She kept him in this beautiful ornate brass cage right next to the window where she grew African violets. I've never had any luck with African violets myself. I always seem to overwater them, and then the leaves get all mushy and rot. I really don't think I could say if one happens more often than the other. I'm just not sure. It feels like every day is a mix of both, really. Buster is a big boy, about seventy-five pounds, so I suppose he's getting up there in age, just like me. My knees certainly feel like they're in dog years sometimes... especially when it rains. The weatherman said it's supposed to rain all weekend, which is a shame because the neighbor's boy was going to come over and help me rake the leaves...\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, before you turned twelve, did you have trouble staying seated or finishing your schoolwork because you were constantly moving or distracted?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. She had them folded up in this old floral tin box that originally held Scottish shortbread cookies. Oh, I absolutely love shortbread cookies, don't you? They are just wonderful with a good cup of tea in the afternoon. She can't even eat a hint of onion without getting awful heartburn. We went to that new Italian place on Main Street for lunch last week, and she made the waiter go back to the kitchen three times to make absolutely sure there wasn't any garlic or onion in her marinara sauce. I felt so awful for the young man, he looked like he was only about sixteen and just terrified of her...\n\nDoctor: When you think about your daily life now, would you say that you struggle with both being easily distracted and being physically restless, or is one of those two problems significantly more frequent than the other?\n\nPatient: Well, I think... didn't we just... well, it really is both, just like I was saying before. I kept getting up to fiddle with the thermostat. My son installed one of those new digital ones for me—he means well, bless his heart, but the numbers are so tiny! I have to hunt down my reading glasses just to turn the heat up two degrees. Anyway, I couldn't sit still through the local broadcast, and then I completely missed the weather report because I got distracted by a smudge on the television screen. I tried to wipe it off with a tissue, but you know those tissues with the aloe lotion in them? They leave the most terrible streaks on glass. Oh, I used to love blowing dandelion seeds when I was a little girl. We had a whole field of them behind our house in Ohio. My sister Margaret and I would make little flower crowns out of them before they went to seed. Margaret lives in Florida now, near Sarasota. She says the humidity is terrible for her arthritis, but she loves being near the ocean...\n\nDoctor: If you had to choose just one, which do you feel causes more significant problems in your daily life: the struggle to focus and keep track of your responsibilities, or the constant need to be moving and the tendency to interrupt others?\n\nPatient: Oh, I really couldn't say. Choosing just one... I don't think I can. They both just cause their own sorts of messes, so I really don't know which one is worse. It's a bit like comparing apples and oranges, isn't it? \n\nOh, speaking of oranges, I bought a bag of those lovely little clementines at the market on Tuesday. You know, the ones that come in those little wooden crates? I always save those crates because they're just so perfect for organizing things. I tried using one to sort my sewing thread last week. I have so many spools of thread, mostly leftover from when I used to make my daughter's Halloween costumes. She was a ladybug one year, with these big red wings that I sewed black felt spots onto. Felt is just so expensive nowadays, though. I went to the craft store last month and nearly had a heart attack at the prices. And the line at the checkout took forever because the cashier's machine ran out of receipt paper...\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these difficulties happen at home and at the community center; do you find that you have these same struggles with focus and restlessness when you are in other situations as well, such as when you are out running errands, visiting friends, or attending appointments?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... yes, it happens everywhere, really. When I'm out running errands or... well, just last week I was at the dentist's office for my regular cleaning. Dr. Miller is such a nice man, but his waiting room chairs are terribly uncomfortable. They're that stiff vinyl material that sticks to the back of your legs if you're wearing a skirt. I was trying to read a magazine while I waited—one of those home decorating ones—but I just couldn't keep my mind on the article about window treatments. I kept getting up to look at the fish tank they have in the corner. They have this one little yellow fish that just darts around endlessly. It reminded me of a canary my grandmother used to keep in her kitchen. She named him Peep, which I always thought was a bit unoriginal for a bird, don't you think? She kept him in this beautiful ornate brass cage right next to the window where she grew African violets. I've never had any luck with African violets myself. I always seem to overwater them, and then the leaves get all mushy and rot. It's so disappointing when you try so hard to take care of a plant and it just dies anyway...\n\nDoctor: If you look back at the past six months, would you say that you have experienced more days where you were primarily struggling to pay attention and stay on task, or more days where you were primarily struggling with being physically restless and unable to control your impulses?\n\nPatient: Well, looking back over the last six months... I really don't think I could say if one happens more often than the other. I'm just not sure. It feels like every day is a mix of both, really. Some days I can't focus, some days I can't sit still, and most days it's a bit of everything. \n\nLike yesterday, for instance. I was trying to sit down and finally write out my Christmas cards. I know it's a bit early, but if I don't start now, they'll never get sent out in time. I bought these beautiful cards from a little boutique downtown—they have a lovely snowy village scene on the front with little specks of glitter that catch the light. But the glitter gets absolutely everywhere! I found a piece on Buster's nose this morning. He looked so funny, just sniffing around with a sparkly nose. He loves the snow, you know. Whenever it snows, he just buries his whole face in it and runs around the yard like a puppy, even though he's eight years old now. Dog years are so strange to think about. I read somewhere that the whole \"seven years for every human year\" thing isn't actually accurate, and it depends on the breed and size of the dog. Buster is a big boy, about seventy-five pounds, so I suppose he's getting up there in age, just like me. My knees certainly feel like they're in dog years sometimes... especially when it rains. The weatherman said it's supposed to rain all weekend, which is a shame because the neighbor's boy was going to come over and help me rake the leaves...\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, before you turned twelve, did you have trouble staying seated or finishing your schoolwork because you were constantly moving or distracted?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. Even way back in grade school, my teachers always told my mother that I was bright but just so scattered. I was always losing my papers, talking out of turn, and just wiggling around in my seat before I ever finished my work. My third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she had this massive beehive hairdo that must have taken a whole can of hairspray to keep up every morning—she was always sending little notes home to my mother about my fidgeting. \n\nMy mother actually kept all those notes. She had them folded up in this old floral tin box that originally held Scottish shortbread cookies. Oh, I absolutely love shortbread cookies, don't you? They are just wonderful with a good cup of tea in the afternoon. I usually have Earl Grey, but my neighbor Brenda insists that peppermint tea is the only thing you should drink after lunch for your digestion. Brenda has terrible digestion, poor thing. She can't even eat a hint of onion without getting awful heartburn. We went to that new Italian place on Main Street for lunch last week, and she made the waiter go back to the kitchen three times to make absolutely sure there wasn't any garlic or onion in her marinara sauce. I felt so awful for the young man, he looked like he was only about sixteen and just terrified of her...\n\nDoctor: When you think about your daily life now, would you say that you struggle with both being easily distracted and being physically restless, or is one of those two problems significantly more frequent than the other?\n\nPatient: Well, I think... didn't we just... well, it really is both, just like I was saying before. I couldn't possibly pick one over the other. It’s just a jumble of both every single day. \n\nLike this morning, I finally sat down in my armchair to watch the morning news, but I just couldn't stay put. I kept getting up to fiddle with the thermostat. My son installed one of those new digital ones for me—he means well, bless his heart, but the numbers are so tiny! I have to hunt down my reading glasses just to turn the heat up two degrees. Anyway, I couldn't sit still through the local broadcast, and then I completely missed the weather report because I got distracted by a smudge on the television screen. I tried to wipe it off with a tissue, but you know those tissues with the aloe lotion in them? They leave the most terrible streaks on glass. I only buy them because my nose gets so raw from my allergies. The goldenrod is just awful right now. Brenda, my neighbor, she actually put artificial flowers out on her front porch just to avoid the pollen. But honestly, I think fake flowers just look a bit tacky, and they collect so much dust. Have you ever tried to dust a fake plastic fern? It’s practically impossible. You have to take the whole thing into the shower and rinse it off, and then the water gets all over the bathroom floor...\n\nDoctor: Are there other specific settings, such as at a doctor's office, a friend's house, or a social gathering, where these struggles with focus and restlessness cause you the same kind of trouble you experience at home?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely, it’s not just sitting in my own living room. It has actually started causing some real strain at home and over at the community center, too. It’s gotten to the point where my husband and the other volunteers just can’t count on me to keep our plans straight without someone checking behind me. \n\nLike just last Tuesday, I was supposed to organize the refreshment table for the morning knitting circle. I used to knit all the time, you know. I made the most beautiful baby blankets whenever someone in the neighborhood was expecting. I always used that soft pastel yellow yarn because you never know if they’re having a boy or a girl, and I think yellow is just such a cheerful color. It reminds me of daffodils. We had a whole row of daffodils along the front walkway at our old house, but the squirrels kept digging up the bulbs! They are such menaces, those squirrels. I tried sprinkling cayenne pepper on the dirt because I read in a magazine that it keeps them away, but the wind just blew it right back into my eyes. Oh, it stung terribly! I had to flush my eyes out with cold water for twenty minutes. \n\nAnyway, my husband gets so frustrated with me now because he feels like he has to supervise me, and the ladies at the center had to scramble to get the coffee made themselves because I was supposed to set it up, but I just kept wandering around the hall looking at the bulletin boards and completely lost track of what I was doing..."
